You see them at the truck stops, and they're just living off those roller hot dogs and the giant fountain sodas, and man, it catches up to them fast. But my wife, God bless her, she's always been a stickler about that. Even long before my dad passed, she was packing my cooler every single night. I get a turkey sandwich on whole wheat, maybe some carrot sticks, an apple, and a thermos of black coffee. It's just... it's like clockwork. \n\nSo even these last three years, with this heavy feeling just sitting on me, my eating hasn't really changed. I mean, there are days when I'm sitting in the cab, looking at that turkey sandwich, and I don't exactly have a... a thrill about eating it. It's just food, you know? It's just fuel for the tank so I can finish the route. But I still eat it. I don't skip my meals, and I haven't been coming home and raiding the pantry at midnight for junk food, either. I still weigh in right around 215. Well, maybe 218 right after Thanksgiving or Christmas, but it always drops right back down by January. My work belt has been on the third notch for a decade. \n\nI actually remember when I first started feeling this way, this whole flat feeling, I thought maybe I just wasn't eating right. Like, maybe I needed a vitamin or something. I even tried eating those chalky protein bars for a couple of weeks instead of the sandwich, thinking maybe it would give me a boost, shake off the cobwebs, but it didn't do a darn thing except make me miss the turkey. So I went back to my regular lunch routine. So, yeah, appetite is fine, weight is exactly the same. I just eat because the clock says it's lunchtime, not because I'm excited for a meal, if that makes sense. But the amount I'm putting away? No, that hasn't changed a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty about things, or like you're not worth much, even when you're doing everything right?\n\nPatient: Well, guilt... I don't know if guilt is exactly the right word for it, but that part about not feeling like you're worth much? Yeah, that... that hits pretty close to home. \n\nI mean, I mentioned my dad earlier, right? The guy who worked the docks for forty years. He was just a mountain of a man. He raised us, paid off the house, put food on the table, and he never complained. Not once. Even when you could see him wincing just trying to get out of his armchair at the end of a shift, he just did what he had to do and took pride in it. And lately—well, for the last three years really—I just keep comparing myself to the kind of man my dad was. \n\nAnd it’s tough, because look, I go to work. I do my job. My wife thinks I’m doing fine, and my boss at the distributor, he never has a bad word to say about my routes or my delivery times. I'm dependable. So it’s not like... nobody’s telling me I’m failing. I don't have people pulling me aside saying I'm a disappointment or that I'm messing up. But man, I hear it in my own head all the time. It’s like this constant, nagging voice telling me that at fifty years old, I should be further along than this. I should have moved up to management by now, or maybe started my own logistics thing, or... I don't know, just made more of myself instead of just hauling kegs and cases the same as I did in my twenties. \n\nAnd even with just the day-to-day stuff, I feel like I'm falling short. Like, my dad wouldn't have let this heavy, flat feeling get to him, you know? He would have just shaken it off and found a way to enjoy his Sunday football. So even when I'm doing everything right on paper—paying the bills, running the routes, eating the lunch my wife packs—I just hear it in my own head that I should be handling things better than this. It’s just this constant sense that I'm not measuring up to the man I was supposed to be, even if nobody else sees it that way. It really wears you down, just feeling like you're a day late and a dollar short in your own life.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of time when your low mood was so severe that it interfered with your ability to work or take care of daily tasks, or was it always more of a constant, low-level struggle that you could manage with effort?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's the thing. It's definitely more of that constant, low-level struggle you mentioned. I haven't... I haven't had days where I just couldn't get out of bed at all, or where I called in sick for a week straight or anything like that. I’m still showing up for work every single morning. I still punch the clock, I still run my routes, and I'm still doing my part around the house, making sure the bills are paid and the pantry is stocked. Like I said, I'm dependable. My dad would've rolled over in his grave if I just stopped showing up to work. \n\nBut man, it's... it's just that everything takes so much more pushing than it used to. It’s like trying to drive a fully loaded rig up a steep grade with the parking brake engaged. You're moving forward, sure, but the engine is screaming, and you're just exhausted by the time you reach the top. Every little thing, from lacing up my work boots in the morning at 4:30 AM to filling out the delivery logs at the end of the shift, it just takes this... this monumental effort that wasn't there before. I have to really talk myself into doing things that used to just be automatic.\n\nAnd it's actually starting to hold me back, to be honest with you. Like I mentioned earlier about feeling like I should be further along, maybe in management? Well, a couple of months ago, a logistics supervisor position actually opened up at the distributor. Old man Peterson finally retired, and the spot was just sitting there. My boss even mentioned it to me in passing, said I knew the routes and the clients better than anyone. So, I printed out the application. I brought it home, sat down at the kitchen table with a pen, and... I just couldn't do it. I got halfway through the first page, filling out my work history, and I just stared at it. \n\nI’ve left that logistics supervisor application sitting unfinished in my desk drawer ever since. I look at it sometimes, but I just don’t trust myself to keep up with it, you know? If it takes this much mental energy just to get through my regular, predictable daily route, how on earth am I going to handle managing other drivers, dealing with dispatch emergencies, and coordinating the warehouse? I'd drop the ball. I just know I would. I don't have the gas in the tank for it right now. So, I just keep doing what I'm doing, managing the day-to-day with sheer effort, but I'm definitely not operating at a hundred percent. It's just a constant, heavy push every single day, just to stay exactly where I am.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt like life wasn't worth living, or had thoughts about not wanting to go on anymore?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, doc, no. I've never... it's never gotten to that point. Not at all. I mean, don't get me wrong, like I've been telling you, it's a grind. It's a real heavy grind every single day, just putting one foot in front of the other, climbing up into the cab, staring at the highway lines. Sometimes I look out the windshield and just think, 'Man, is this all there is? Just miles and miles of blacktop until I retire?' 

But... but actually wanting to not be here? Or thinking about doing something to... you know, end it? No, absolutely not. I couldn't ever do that. For one thing, there's my wife. Like I said about the lunches she packs—she relies on me. We've got the house, we've got the mortgage, and she works part-time over at the pharmacy, but my paycheck is what really keeps the lights on and the heat running. If I just decided I was done, if I just checked out, what would happen to her? I couldn't leave her in a lurch like that. It wouldn't be right. 

And then there's just... I guess it goes back to how I was raised, you know? With my dad. You don't just quit. You don't just abandon your post because you're tired or because you're not feeling a hundred percent. You punch the clock. Even on the days when the engine is screaming and the parking brake is on, like I was saying before, you still drive the truck. You still make the deliveries. You don't just walk off the job, and you sure don't walk off the job of living. So, no, I don't have those kinds of thoughts. It's just a lack of joy, I guess, not a lack of... wanting to be alive, if you see the difference. I'm here for the long haul, even if the haul feels a heck of a lot longer and heavier these days.

It's just this constant, low-grade weight that never really takes a day off. I keep thinking maybe if I take a different route, or if I sleep in on a Sunday, it'll lift, but it just... it just stays. It's like a hum in the background that you can't turn off, no matter what room you walk into.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy levels, feelings of worthlessness, or difficulty concentrating along with that heavy feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, energy-wise, I'm getting by, I suppose. I know I'm doing a good job, I know my customers rely on me, and I know the guys at the hall appreciate the work I put in. I used to be able to glance at a route sheet once and have my whole morning mapped out in my head. Now I'm double-checking, triple-checking, just to make sure I didn't miss a change in the schedule. It's frustrating, doc. It's just another part of that whole heavy, dull feeling, I guess. I mean, I'm not pacing the floors or anything like that. I'm not tapping my foot or feeling like I need to jump out of my skin. And I wouldn't say I'm moving like molasses, either. So, yeah, I'm not sprinting across the parking lot, but I'm moving at my regular pace. \n\nI still get out of the truck, unlatch the doors, pull the hand truck down, and wheel the product into the stores just fine. I mean, maybe it takes a little more mental push to get myself out of the seat sometimes, just because of that heavy feeling I was talking about, but physically? No. I'm not dragging my feet or moving in slow motion. And I can sit still just fine. Honestly, sitting in the cab with the radio on is about the only time I feel somewhat settled. So, no, I haven't really noticed any of that restlessness or moving super slow. It's more just... it's all in my head, you know? Just that dullness. But my body is still doing what it needs to do at the speed it usually does it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with that heavy, low feeling and loss of enjoyment in things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's the thing. It's been a long haul. It's not like I woke up a couple of weeks ago and just felt off. No, it's been... gosh, it's been about three years now. Yeah, three years. \n\nI know the timeline pretty exactly because it all started around the time my father passed away. He was a good man, worked on the docks down at the port for forty years. Tough as nails, but he taught me everything I know about a hard day's work. When he went, it was... well, it was rough. We knew it was coming, his heart had been giving out on him for a while, but you're never really ready, you know? And after the funeral, and settling the estate—which wasn't much, just the house and his old Buick—and getting my mom situated, I just figured I was grieving. Everyone said, \"Take your time, it's a process, you'll bounce back.\" \n\nAnd I waited to bounce back. I kept driving my routes, kept going to the hall, kept doing what I was supposed to do, thinking that eventually, the fog would lift. I kept telling myself, \"Give it six months,\" and then, \"Give it a year.\" But the year came and went, and then another year, and now it's been three years. Three whole years since he died, and that heavy feeling, that lead vest I was telling you about? It just never came off. \n\nAt first, I thought it was just the grief, just missing the old man. We used to watch the Sunday games together, and for a long time, I couldn't even turn the TV on without feeling a lump in my throat. But then, even when the sharp pain of losing him started to dull a bit, this other thing, this low-grade hum, it just stayed. It settled in. It's like the grief just kind of morphed into this permanent state of... I don't know, just feeling flat. So, yeah, three years. Three years of just pushing through it every single day. It's a long time to feel like you're just treading water, doc.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past few years—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: No, doc, not really. I get a turkey sandwich on whole wheat, maybe some carrot sticks, an apple, and a thermos of black coffee. It's just... it's like clockwork. \n\nSo even these last three years, with this heavy feeling just sitting on me, my eating hasn't really changed. I mean, there are days when I'm sitting in the cab, looking at that turkey sandwich, and I don't exactly have a... a thrill about eating it. It's just food, you know? It's just fuel for the tank so I can finish the route. But I still eat it. I don't skip my meals, and I haven't been coming home and raiding the pantry at midnight for junk food, either. I still weigh in right around 215. Well, maybe 218 right after Thanksgiving or Christmas, but it always drops right back down by January. My work belt has been on the third notch for a decade. \n\nI actually remember when I first started feeling this way, this whole flat feeling, I thought maybe I just wasn't eating right. Like, maybe I needed a vitamin or something. I even tried eating those chalky protein bars for a couple of weeks instead of the sandwich, thinking maybe it would give me a boost, shake off the cobwebs, but it didn't do a darn thing except make me miss the turkey. So I went back to my regular lunch routine. So, yeah, appetite is fine, weight is exactly the same. I just eat because the clock says it's lunchtime, not because I'm excited for a meal, if that makes sense. But the amount I'm putting away? No, that hasn't changed a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty about things, or like you're not worth much, even when you're doing everything right?\n\nPatient: Well, guilt... I don't know if guilt is exactly the right word for it, but that part about not feeling like you're worth much? Yeah, that... that hits pretty close to home. \n\nI mean, I mentioned my dad earlier, right? The guy who worked the docks for forty years. He was just a mountain of a man. He raised us, paid off the house, put food on the table, and he never complained. Not once. Even when you could see him wincing just trying to get out of his armchair at the end of a shift, he just did what he had to do and took pride in it. And lately—well, for the last three years really—I just keep comparing myself to the kind of man my dad was. \n\nAnd it’s tough, because look, I go to work. I do my job. My wife thinks I’m doing fine, and my boss at the distributor, he never has a bad word to say about my routes or my delivery times. I'm dependable. So it’s not like... nobody’s telling me I’m failing. I don't have people pulling me aside saying I'm a disappointment or that I'm messing up. But man, I hear it in my own head all the time. It’s like this constant, nagging voice telling me that at fifty years old, I should be further along than this. I should have moved up to management by now, or maybe started my own logistics thing, or... I don't know, just made more of myself instead of just hauling kegs and cases the same as I did in my twenties. \n\nAnd even with just the day-to-day stuff, I feel like I'm falling short. Like, my dad wouldn't have let this heavy, flat feeling get to him, you know? He would have just shaken it off and found a way to enjoy his Sunday football. So even when I'm doing everything right on paper—paying the bills, running the routes, eating the lunch my wife packs—I just hear it in my own head that I should be handling things better than this. It’s just this constant sense that I'm not measuring up to the man I was supposed to be, even if nobody else sees it that way. It really wears you down, just feeling like you're a day late and a dollar short in your own life.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of time when your low mood was so severe that it interfered with your ability to work or take care of daily tasks, or was it always more of a constant, low-level struggle that you could manage with effort?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's the thing. It's definitely more of that constant, low-level struggle you mentioned. I haven't... I haven't had days where I just couldn't get out of bed at all, or where I called in sick for a week straight or anything like that. I’m still showing up for work every single morning. I still punch the clock, I still run my routes, and I'm still doing my part around the house, making sure the bills are paid and the pantry is stocked. Like I said, I'm dependable. My dad would've rolled over in his grave if I just stopped showing up to work. \n\nBut man, it's... it's just that everything takes so much more pushing than it used to. It’s like trying to drive a fully loaded rig up a steep grade with the parking brake engaged. You're moving forward, sure, but the engine is screaming, and you're just exhausted by the time you reach the top. Every little thing, from lacing up my work boots in the morning at 4:30 AM to filling out the delivery logs at the end of the shift, it just takes this... this monumental effort that wasn't there before. I have to really talk myself into doing things that used to just be automatic.\n\nAnd it's actually starting to hold me back, to be honest with you. Like I mentioned earlier about feeling like I should be further along, maybe in management? Well, a couple of months ago, a logistics supervisor position actually opened up at the distributor. Old man Peterson finally retired, and the spot was just sitting there. My boss even mentioned it to me in passing, said I knew the routes and the clients better than anyone. So, I printed out the application. I brought it home, sat down at the kitchen table with a pen, and... I just couldn't do it. I got halfway through the first page, filling out my work history, and I just stared at it. \n\nI’ve left that logistics supervisor application sitting unfinished in my desk drawer ever since. I look at it sometimes, but I just don’t trust myself to keep up with it, you know? If it takes this much mental energy just to get through my regular, predictable daily route, how on earth am I going to handle managing other drivers, dealing with dispatch emergencies, and coordinating the warehouse? I'd drop the ball. I just know I would. I don't have the gas in the tank for it right now. So, I just keep doing what I'm doing, managing the day-to-day with sheer effort, but I'm definitely not operating at a hundred percent. It's just a constant, heavy push every single day, just to stay exactly where I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life wasn't worth living, or had thoughts about not wanting to go on anymore?\n\nPatient: No, doc, no. I've never... it's never gotten to that point. Not at all. I mean, don't get me wrong, like I've been telling you, it's a grind. It's a real heavy grind every single day, just putting one foot in front of the other, climbing up into the cab, staring at the highway lines. Sometimes I look out the windshield and just think, 'Man, is this all there is? Just miles and miles of blacktop until I retire?' \n\nBut... but actually wanting to not be here? Or thinking about doing something to... you know, end it? No, absolutely not. I couldn't ever do that. For one thing, there's my wife. Like I said about the lunches she packs—she relies on me. We've got the house, we've got the mortgage, and she works part-time over at the pharmacy, but my paycheck is what really keeps the lights on and the heat running. If I just decided I was done, if I just checked out, what would happen to her? I couldn't leave her in a lurch like that. It wouldn't be right. \n\nAnd then there's just... I guess it goes back to how I was raised, you know? With my dad. You don't just quit. You don't just abandon your post because you're tired or because you're not feeling a hundred percent. You punch the clock. Even on the days when the engine is screaming and the parking brake is on, like I was saying before, you still drive the truck. You still make the deliveries. You don't just walk off the job, and you sure don't walk off the job of living. So, no, I don't have those kinds of thoughts. It's just a lack of joy, I guess, not a lack of... wanting to be alive, if you see the difference. I'm here for the long haul, even if the haul feels a heck of a lot longer and heavier these days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

  }

I don't know if guilt is exactly the right word for it, but that part about not feeling like you're worth much? He raised us, paid off the house, put food on the table, and he never complained. Not once. Even when you could see him wincing just trying to get out of his armchair at the end of a shift, he just did what he had to do and took pride in it. And lately—well, for the last three years really—I just keep comparing myself to the kind of man my dad was. \n\nAnd it’s tough, because look, I go to work. I do my job. My wife thinks I’m doing fine, and my boss at the distributor, he never has a bad word to say about my routes or my delivery times. I'm dependable. So it’s not like... nobody’s telling me I’m failing. I don't have people pulling me aside saying I'm a disappointment or that I'm messing up. But man, I hear it in my own head all the time. It’s like this constant, nagging voice telling me that at fifty years old, I should be further along than this. I should have moved up to management by now, or maybe started my own logistics thing, or... I don't know, just made more of myself instead of just hauling kegs and cases the same as I did in my twenties. \n\nAnd even with just the day-to-day stuff, I feel like I'm falling short. Like, my dad wouldn't have let this heavy, flat feeling get to him, you know? He would have just shaken it off and found a way to enjoy his Sunday football. So even when I'm doing everything right on paper—paying the bills, running the routes, eating the lunch my wife packs—I just hear it in my own head that I should be handling things better than this. It’s just this constant sense that I'm not measuring up to the man I was supposed to be, even if nobody else sees it that way. It really wears you down, just feeling like you're a day late and a dollar short in your own life.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of time when your low mood was so severe that it interfered with your ability to work or take care of daily tasks, or was it always more of a constant, low-level struggle that you could manage with effort?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's the thing. It's definitely more of that constant, low-level struggle you mentioned. I haven't... I haven't had days where I just couldn't get out of bed at all, or where I called in sick for a week straight or anything like that. I’m still showing up for work every single morning. I still punch the clock, I still run my routes, and I'm still doing my part around the house, making sure the bills are paid and the pantry is stocked. Like I said, I'm dependable. My dad would've rolled over in his grave if I just stopped showing up to work. \n\nBut man, it's... it's just that everything takes so much more pushing than it used to. It’s like trying to drive a fully loaded rig up a steep grade with the parking brake engaged. You're moving forward, sure, but the engine is screaming, and you're just exhausted by the time you reach the top. Every little thing, from lacing up my work boots in the morning at 4:30 AM to filling out the delivery logs at the end of the shift, it just takes this... this monumental effort that wasn't there before. I have to really talk myself into doing things that used to just be automatic.\n\nAnd it's actually starting to hold me back, to be honest with you. Like I mentioned earlier about feeling like I should be further along, maybe in management? Well, a couple of months ago, a logistics supervisor position actually opened up at the distributor. Old man Peterson finally retired, and the spot was just sitting there. My boss even mentioned it to me in passing, said I knew the routes and the clients better than anyone. So, I printed out the application. I brought it home, sat down at the kitchen table with a pen, and... I just couldn't do it. I got halfway through the first page, filling out my work history, and I just stared at it. \n\nI’ve left that logistics supervisor application sitting unfinished in my desk drawer ever since. I look at it sometimes, but I just don’t trust myself to keep up with it, you know? If it takes this much mental energy just to get through my regular, predictable daily route, how on earth am I going to handle managing other drivers, dealing with dispatch emergencies, and coordinating the warehouse? I'd drop the ball. I just know I would. I don't have the gas in the tank for it right now. So, I just keep doing what I'm doing, managing the day-to-day with sheer effort, but I'm definitely not operating at a hundred percent. It's just a constant, heavy push every single day, just to stay exactly where I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life wasn't worth living, or had thoughts about not wanting to go on anymore?\n\nPatient: No, doc, no. I've never... it's never gotten to that point. Not at all. I mean, don't get me wrong, like I've been telling you, it's a grind. It's a real heavy grind every single day, just putting one foot in front of the other, climbing up into the cab, staring at the highway lines. Sometimes I look out the windshield and just think, 'Man, is this all there is? Just miles and miles of blacktop until I retire?' \n\nBut... but actually wanting to not be here? Or thinking about doing something to... you know, end it? No, absolutely not. I couldn't ever do that. For one thing, there's my wife. Like I said about the lunches she packs—she relies on me. We've got the house, we've got the mortgage, and she works part-time over at the pharmacy, but my paycheck is what really keeps the lights on and the heat running. If I just decided I was done, if I just checked out, what would happen to her? I couldn't leave her in a lurch like that. It wouldn't be right. \n\nAnd then there's just... I guess it goes back to how I was raised, you know? With my dad. You don't just quit. You don't just abandon your post because you're tired or because you're not feeling a hundred percent. You punch the clock. Even on the days when the engine is screaming and the parking brake is on, like I was saying before, you still drive the truck. You still make the deliveries. You don't just walk off the job, and you sure don't walk off the job of living. So, no, I don't have those kinds of thoughts. It's just a lack of joy, I guess, not a lack of... wanting to be alive, if you see the difference. I'm here for the long haul, even if the haul feels a heck of a lot longer and heavier these days.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had times when your mood got significantly worse for a while, even if it later improved a little, or has it always been about the same level of heaviness throughout the past three years?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, to be honest with you, doc, it's really just been this one long, steady, flat line of heavy. I haven't really had these big sudden drops where it gets a lot worse and then bounces back up. It’s more like... well, it’s been exactly this same way for about three years now. And the reason I know the timeline so well, the reason I know it's been three years, is because... well, that's when my dad died. 

I know I’ve been talking about him a lot today, about the kind of man he was and how he worked the docks, but yeah, he passed away three years ago. And ever since then, ever since we put him in the ground, this feeling just kind of moved in and set up shop. I remember right after the funeral, I took maybe three days off work. Just three. Because, like I told you, he wouldn't have wanted me sitting around the house feeling sorry for myself. He worked right up until the end, so I figured the best way to honor him was to get back to the route. 

So I went back. I climbed up into the cab that first Monday morning, turned the key, and I just felt this... this massive weight settle right on my shoulders. At first, I just figured it was normal, you know? Just the grief. I thought it would lift after a few months, maybe after we got his house cleaned out and sold his old pickup and got the estate all squared away. You expect it to be hard at first. But the thing is... it just never lifted. It never changed. It hasn't gotten any worse than that first day back in the truck, but it sure hasn't gotten any better, either. It’s just been this exact same level of heaviness, every single day, for three solid years since we lost him. You just wake up, you feel the weight, you think about him, and you force yourself to push through it. So no, no big dips or rollercoasters. Just one long, flat, heavy road since the day he died.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.1, F33.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. Can you tell me what’s been bringing you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, thanks for seeing me, doc. I, uh... I guess I came in because, well, it's been going on for a really long time now. I drive a truck for a living, doing sales and deliveries, and, you know, I get up every morning, I do my pre-trip, I get through my routes just fine. I even still make it to my volunteer shifts on the weekends, setting up the hall and all that. So, on the outside, I'm doing everything I'm supposed to do, paying the bills and showing up. \n\nBut... inside, there's just this—this heavy, low feeling sitting on me. Almost every single day. It’s hard to explain, really. It’s like I’m just going through the motions of it all instead of really being in my own life. I’ll be sitting up in the cab, looking out at the highway, listening to the radio, and I just feel weighed down by this... this dullness, I guess you could call it. It just sits right on top of me. And it's been like this for so long that I almost got used to it, just waking up and putting one foot in front of the other. But I just thought, you know, I'm 50 years old now, I shouldn't have to spend the rest of my life feeling like a ghost just steering a wheel. I'm doing the work, I'm showing up for people, but that heaviness just never really lifts off me. It just feels like I'm on autopilot, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any loss of interest or pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, even things you still do regularly like your volunteer work?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the funny thing about it, doc. I wouldn't say I've lost interest, exactly. I mean, like I said, I still go down to the hall every weekend. I still set up the chairs, make the coffee, talk to the guys. I used to really look forward to it, you know? It was the highlight of my week, getting to catch up with everybody, feeling like I was part of something bigger than just driving a route all week. And I still do it, I still show up. But it's just... it's like the color's been drained out of it. \n\nI'm there, I'm doing the things, I'm smiling when I'm supposed to smile, but I just don't feel that same spark I used to. It's not like I hate it now, or I want to quit. I don't. I just... I don't get that same kick out of it. Same goes for when I'm on the road. I used to love finding a new diner, trying out a different route, listening to a new podcast. Now it's just... it's just driving. It's just getting from point A to point B. I don't know if that's losing interest or if it's just getting older, but it definitely feels different. It's like I'm eating the same food I always loved, but I can't taste it anymore. And it's not just the volunteering or the driving, it's everything. It's like this blanket is just smothering the joy out of everything I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel low or depressed most of the time, almost every day, regardless of what you're doing or where you are?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, doc, that's exactly it. That's... that's the whole reason I finally decided to make this appointment, to be honest. It's like I was saying before, I'm getting through my routes, I'm doing the sales, I'm showing up for my volunteer shifts down at the hall—I haven't missed a weekend in I don't know how long—but underneath all of that, no matter where I am or what I'm doing, there's just this... this heavy, low feeling. And it's there most days. Almost every day, really. \n\nIt doesn't matter if I'm having a good run on the highway with clear weather, or if I'm sitting at home trying to watch a ballgame, or if I'm pouring coffee for the guys on a Saturday morning. It's just sitting right there on me. It's like I'm wearing a lead vest under my shirt that nobody else can see. I'm going through the motions of my life—I'm shifting the gears, I'm checking the manifests, I'm paying the electric bill—but I'm not really *in* it. It's just this constant, low-grade weight that never really takes a day off. I keep thinking maybe if I take a different route, or if I sleep in on a Sunday, it'll lift, but it just... it just stays. It's like a hum in the background that you can't turn off, no matter what room you walk into.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy levels, feelings of worthlessness, or difficulty concentrating along with that heavy feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, energy-wise, I'm getting by, I suppose. I mean, I'm not running marathons, but I'm getting up at 4:30 AM like I always do, doing my pre-trip inspection, checking the tires, the oil, making sure the load is secured. I don't feel completely wiped out or anything, and I don't really feel worthless, you know? I know I'm doing a good job, I know my customers rely on me, and I know the guys at the hall appreciate the work I put in. I'm a steady guy. \n\nBut... the concentration thing, that's... that's been bugging me a bit lately. It's not like I'm forgetting how to drive or anything dangerous like that. I've been driving this same rig for eight years, I could probably back it into a loading dock blindfolded. But it's the paperwork, the little details. I'll be looking at a manifest, right? And I know I need to be at the warehouse on 4th Street by 9:15 AM, and then the hardware store over in Westside by 11:00. But I'll catch myself just... staring at the paper. I'll read the delivery time, and then I'll look away, and I have to look right back at it because it just didn't stick in my head. \n\nIt's like my brain is just coated in syrup or something. I'll be standing in front of the dispatch board in the morning, holding my coffee, and I'll just be looking at it way longer than I should need to. The letters and numbers are all there, I'm reading them, but my mind just doesn't... lock onto them the way it used to. I used to be able to glance at a route sheet once and have my whole morning mapped out in my head. Now I'm double-checking, triple-checking, just to make sure I didn't miss a change in the schedule. It's frustrating, doc. I can still do the job, I haven't missed a delivery or messed up an order, but it takes this extra... this extra effort just to keep my thoughts from slipping away like water off a tarp. It's just another part of that whole heavy, dull feeling, I guess. It just slows everything down up there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless inside?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I'm not pacing the floors or anything like that. I'm not tapping my foot or feeling like I need to jump out of my skin. And I wouldn't say I'm moving like molasses, either. I mean, I'm a big guy, and I've got a bad knee from jumping out of the cab too many times back in the nineties when I drove for that beverage distributor—man, those kegs were heavy, and we didn't have the lift gates we do now. So, yeah, I'm not sprinting across the parking lot, but I'm moving at my regular pace. \n\nI still get out of the truck, unlatch the doors, pull the hand truck down, and wheel the product into the stores just fine. I mean, maybe it takes a little more mental push to get myself out of the seat sometimes, just because of that heavy feeling I was talking about, but physically? No. I'm not dragging my feet or moving in slow motion. And I can sit still just fine. Honestly, sitting in the cab with the radio on is about the only time I feel somewhat settled. So, no, I haven't really noticed any of that restlessness or moving super slow. It's more just... it's all in my head, you know? Just that dullness. But my body is still doing what it needs to do at the speed it usually does it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with that heavy, low feeling and loss of enjoyment in things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's the thing. It's been a long haul. It's not like I woke up a couple of weeks ago and just felt off. No, it's been... gosh, it's been about three years now. Yeah, three years. \n\nI know the timeline pretty exactly because it all started around the time my father passed away. He was a good man, worked on the docks down at the port for forty years. Tough as nails, but he taught me everything I know about a hard day's work. When he went, it was... well, it was rough. We knew it was coming, his heart had been giving out on him for a while, but you're never really ready, you know? And after the funeral, and settling the estate—which wasn't much, just the house and his old Buick—and getting my mom situated, I just figured I was grieving. Everyone said, \"Take your time, it's a process, you'll bounce back.\" \n\nAnd I waited to bounce back. I kept driving my routes, kept going to the hall, kept doing what I was supposed to do, thinking that eventually, the fog would lift. I kept telling myself, \"Give it six months,\" and then, \"Give it a year.\" But the year came and went, and then another year, and now it's been three years. Three whole years since he died, and that heavy feeling, that lead vest I was telling you about? It just never came off. \n\nAt first, I thought it was just the grief, just missing the old man. We used to watch the Sunday games together, and for a long time, I couldn't even turn the TV on without feeling a lump in my throat. But then, even when the sharp pain of losing him started to dull a bit, this other thing, this low-grade hum, it just stayed. It settled in. It's like the grief just kind of morphed into this permanent state of... I don't know, just feeling flat. So, yeah, three years. Three years of just pushing through it every single day. It's a long time to feel like you're just treading water, doc.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past few years—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: No, doc, not really. My weight's been pretty much exactly the same since I hit my thirties, to be honest with you. You'd think, being behind the wheel all day, sitting in that cab for hours on end, that I'd be packing on the pounds. A lot of the guys do, you know? You see them at the truck stops, and they're just living off those roller hot dogs and the giant fountain sodas, and man, it catches up to them fast. But my wife, God bless her, she's always been a stickler about that. Even long before my dad passed, she was packing my cooler every single night. I get a turkey sandwich on whole wheat, maybe some carrot sticks, an apple, and a thermos of black coffee. It's just... it's like clockwork. \n\nSo even these last three years, with this heavy feeling just sitting on me, my eating hasn't really changed. I mean, there are days when I'm sitting in the cab, looking at that turkey sandwich, and I don't exactly have a... a thrill about eating it. It's just food, you know? It's just fuel for the tank so I can finish the route. But I still eat it. I don't skip my meals, and I haven't been coming home and raiding the pantry at midnight for junk food, either. I still weigh in right around 215. Well, maybe 218 right after Thanksgiving or Christmas, but it always drops right back down by January. My work belt has been on the third notch for a decade. \n\nI actually remember when I first started feeling this way, this whole flat feeling, I thought maybe I just wasn't eating right. Like, maybe I needed a vitamin or something. I even tried eating those chalky protein bars for a couple of weeks instead of the sandwich, thinking maybe it would give me a boost, shake off the cobwebs, but it didn't do a darn thing except make me miss the turkey. So I went back to my regular lunch routine. So, yeah, appetite is fine, weight is exactly the same. I just eat because the clock says it's lunchtime, not because I'm excited for a meal, if that makes sense. But the amount I'm putting away? No, that hasn't changed a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty about things, or like you're not worth much, even when you're doing everything right?\n\nPatient: Well, guilt... I don't know if guilt is exactly the right word for it, but that part about not feeling like you're worth much? Yeah, that... that hits pretty close to home. \n\nI mean, I mentioned my dad earlier, right? The guy who worked the docks for forty years. He was just a mountain of a man. He raised us, paid off the house, put food on the table, and he never complained. Not once. Even when you could see him wincing just trying to get out of his armchair at the end of a shift, he just did what he had to do and took pride in it. And lately—well, for the last three years really—I just keep comparing myself to the kind of man my dad was. \n\nAnd it’s tough, because look, I go to work. I do my job. My wife thinks I’m doing fine, and my boss at the distributor, he never has a bad word to say about my routes or my delivery times. I'm dependable. So it’s not like... nobody’s telling me I’m failing. I don't have people pulling me aside saying I'm a disappointment or that I'm messing up. But man, I hear it in my own head all the time. It’s like this constant, nagging voice telling me that at fifty years old, I should be further along than this. I should have moved up to management by now, or maybe started my own logistics thing, or... I don't know, just made more of myself instead of just hauling kegs and cases the same as I did in my twenties. \n\nAnd even with just the day-to-day stuff, I feel like I'm falling short. Like, my dad wouldn't have let this heavy, flat feeling get to him, you know? He would have just shaken it off and found a way to enjoy his Sunday football. So even when I'm doing everything right on paper—paying the bills, running the routes, eating the lunch my wife packs—I just hear it in my own head that I should be handling things better than this. It’s just this constant sense that I'm not measuring up to the man I was supposed to be, even if nobody else sees it that way. It really wears you down, just feeling like you're a day late and a dollar short in your own life.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of time when your low mood was so severe that it interfered with your ability to work or take care of daily tasks, or was it always more of a constant, low-level struggle that you could manage with effort?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's the thing. It's definitely more of that constant, low-level struggle you mentioned. I haven't... I haven't had days where I just couldn't get out of bed at all, or where I called in sick for a week straight or anything like that. I’m still showing up for work every single morning. I still punch the clock, I still run my routes, and I'm still doing my part around the house, making sure the bills are paid and the pantry is stocked. Like I said, I'm dependable. My dad would've rolled over in his grave if I just stopped showing up to work. \n\nBut man, it's... it's just that everything takes so much more pushing than it used to. It’s like trying to drive a fully loaded rig up a steep grade with the parking brake engaged. You're moving forward, sure, but the engine is screaming, and you're just exhausted by the time you reach the top. Every little thing, from lacing up my work boots in the morning at 4:30 AM to filling out the delivery logs at the end of the shift, it just takes this... this monumental effort that wasn't there before. I have to really talk myself into doing things that used to just be automatic.\n\nAnd it's actually starting to hold me back, to be honest with you. Like I mentioned earlier about feeling like I should be further along, maybe in management? Well, a couple of months ago, a logistics supervisor position actually opened up at the distributor. Old man Peterson finally retired, and the spot was just sitting there. My boss even mentioned it to me in passing, said I knew the routes and the clients better than anyone. So, I printed out the application. I brought it home, sat down at the kitchen table with a pen, and... I just couldn't do it. I got halfway through the first page, filling out my work history, and I just stared at it. \n\nI’ve left that logistics supervisor application sitting unfinished in my desk drawer ever since. I look at it sometimes, but I just don’t trust myself to keep up with it, you know? If it takes this much mental energy just to get through my regular, predictable daily route, how on earth am I going to handle managing other drivers, dealing with dispatch emergencies, and coordinating the warehouse? I'd drop the ball. I just know I would. I don't have the gas in the tank for it right now. So, I just keep doing what I'm doing, managing the day-to-day with sheer effort, but I'm definitely not operating at a hundred percent. It's just a constant, heavy push every single day, just to stay exactly where I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life wasn't worth living, or had thoughts about not wanting to go on anymore?\n\nPatient: No, doc, no. I've never... it's never gotten to that point. Not at all. I mean, don't get me wrong, like I've been telling you, it's a grind. It's a real heavy grind every single day, just putting one foot in front of the other, climbing up into the cab, staring at the highway lines. Sometimes I look out the windshield and just think, 'Man, is this all there is? Just miles and miles of blacktop until I retire?' \n\nBut... but actually wanting to not be here? Or thinking about doing something to... you know, end it? No, absolutely not. I couldn't ever do that. For one thing, there's my wife. Like I said about the lunches she packs—she relies on me. We've got the house, we've got the mortgage, and she works part-time over at the pharmacy, but my paycheck is what really keeps the lights on and the heat running. If I just decided I was done, if I just checked out, what would happen to her? I couldn't leave her in a lurch like that. It wouldn't be right. \n\nAnd then there's just... I guess it goes back to how I was raised, you know? With my dad. You don't just quit. You don't just abandon your post because you're tired or because you're not feeling a hundred percent. You punch the clock. Even on the days when the engine is screaming and the parking brake is on, like I was saying before, you still drive the truck. You still make the deliveries. You don't just walk off the job, and you sure don't walk off the job of living. So, no, I don't have those kinds of thoughts. It's just a lack of joy, I guess, not a lack of... wanting to be alive, if you see the difference. I'm here for the long haul, even if the haul feels a heck of a lot longer and heavier these days.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when your mood got significantly worse for a while, even if it later improved a little, or has it always been about the same level of heaviness throughout the past three years?\n\nPatient: Well, to be honest with you, doc, it's really just been this one long, steady, flat line of heavy. I haven't really had these big sudden drops where it gets a lot worse and then bounces back up. It’s more like... well, it’s been exactly this same way for about three years now. And the reason I know the timeline so well, the reason I know it's been three years, is because... well, that's when my dad died. \n\nI know I’ve been talking about him a lot today, about the kind of man he was and how he worked the docks, but yeah, he passed away three years ago. And ever since then, ever since we put him in the ground, this feeling just kind of moved in and set up shop. I remember right after the funeral, I took maybe three days off work. Just three. Because, like I told you, he wouldn't have wanted me sitting around the house feeling sorry for myself. He worked right up until the end, so I figured the best way to honor him was to get back to the route. \n\nSo I went back. I climbed up into the cab that first Monday morning, turned the key, and I just felt this... this massive weight settle right on my shoulders. At first, I just figured it was normal, you know? Just the grief. I thought it would lift after a few months, maybe after we got his house cleaned out and sold his old pickup and got the estate all squared away. You expect it to be hard at first. But the thing is... it just never lifted. It never changed. It hasn't gotten any worse than that first day back in the truck, but it sure hasn't gotten any better, either. It’s just been this exact same level of heaviness, every single day, for three solid years since we lost him. You just wake up, you feel the weight, you think about him, and you force yourself to push through it. So no, no big dips or rollercoasters. Just one long, flat, heavy road since the day he died.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like I mentioned earlier about feeling like I should be further along, maybe in management? Well, a couple of months ago, a logistics supervisor position actually opened up at the distributor. Old man Peterson finally retired, and the spot was just sitting there. My boss even mentioned it to me in passing, said I knew the routes and the clients better than anyone. So, I printed out the application. I brought it home, sat down at the kitchen table with a pen, and... I just couldn't do it. I got halfway through the first page, filling out my work history, and I just stared at it. \n\nI’ve left that logistics supervisor application sitting unfinished in my desk drawer ever since. I look at it sometimes, but I just don’t trust myself to keep up with it, you know? If it takes this much mental energy just to get through my regular, predictable daily route, how on earth am I going to handle managing other drivers, dealing with dispatch emergencies, and coordinating the warehouse? I'd drop the ball. I just know I would. I don't have the gas in the tank for it right now. So, I just keep doing what I'm doing, managing the day-to-day with sheer effort, but I'm definitely not operating at a hundred percent. It's just a constant, heavy push every single day, just to stay exactly where I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life wasn't worth living, or had thoughts about not wanting to go on anymore?\n\nPatient: No, doc, no. I've never... it's never gotten to that point. Not at all. I mean, don't get me wrong, like I've been telling you, it's a grind. It's a real heavy grind every single day, just putting one foot in front of the other, climbing up into the cab, staring at the highway lines. Sometimes I look out the windshield and just think, 'Man, is this all there is? Just miles and miles of blacktop until I retire?' \n\nBut... but actually wanting to not be here? Or thinking about doing something to... you know, end it? No, absolutely not. I couldn't ever do that. For one thing, there's my wife. Like I said about the lunches she packs—she relies on me. We've got the house, we've got the mortgage, and she works part-time over at the pharmacy, but my paycheck is what really keeps the lights on and the heat running. If I just decided I was done, if I just checked out, what would happen to her? I couldn't leave her in a lurch like that. It wouldn't be right. \n\nAnd then there's just... I guess it goes back to how I was raised, you know? With my dad. You don't just quit. You don't just abandon your post because you're tired or because you're not feeling a hundred percent. You punch the clock. Even on the days when the engine is screaming and the parking brake is on, like I was saying before, you still drive the truck. You still make the deliveries. You don't just walk off the job, and you sure don't walk off the job of living. So, no, I don't have those kinds of thoughts. It's just a lack of joy, I guess, not a lack of... wanting to be alive, if you see the difference. I'm here for the long haul, even if the haul feels a heck of a lot longer and heavier these days.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when your mood got significantly worse for a while, even if it later improved a little, or has it always been about the same level of heaviness throughout the past three years?\n\nPatient: Well, to be honest with you, doc, it's really just been this one long, steady, flat line of heavy. I haven't really had these big sudden drops where it gets a lot worse and then bounces back up. It’s more like... well, it’s been exactly this same way for about three years now. And the reason I know the timeline so well, the reason I know it's been three years, is because... well, that's when my dad died. \n\nI know I’ve been talking about him a lot today, about the kind of man he was and how he worked the docks, but yeah, he passed away three years ago. And ever since then, ever since we put him in the ground, this feeling just kind of moved in and set up shop. I remember right after the funeral, I took maybe three days off work. Just three. Because, like I told you, he wouldn't have wanted me sitting around the house feeling sorry for myself. He worked right up until the end, so I figured the best way to honor him was to get back to the route. \n\nSo I went back. I climbed up into the cab that first Monday morning, turned the key, and I just felt this... this massive weight settle right on my shoulders. At first, I just figured it was normal, you know? Just the grief. I thought it would lift after a few months, maybe after we got his house cleaned out and sold his old pickup and got the estate all squared away. You expect it to be hard at first. But the thing is... it just never lifted. It never changed. It hasn't gotten any worse than that first day back in the truck, but it sure hasn't gotten any better, either. It’s just been this exact same level of heaviness, every single day, for three solid years since we lost him. You just wake up, you feel the weight, you think about him, and you force yourself to push through it. So no, no big dips or rollercoasters. Just one long, flat, heavy road since the day he died."
